3:["$","p",null,{"children":"This tutorial walks through a single end-to-end experiment and shows how to combine these tools at each stage of a typical development workflow. You refresh device properties to select a better layout, inspect circuit timing, learn a noise model and use it to preview the benefit of error mitigation with local simulation, estimate QPU usage, recalibrate readout at the start of a session, and finally inspect how your job ran on the hardware. Not every tool is useful for every experiment, so choose the tools that address the questions you have about your own workload.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]

3f:["$","p",null,{"children":"This tutorial uses PEC with TREX directly, so that you can follow along without an AI agent.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
40:["$","p",null,{"children":"PEC is a technique for mitigating quantum noise in observable expectation value calculations. Under ideal conditions, it can produce an unbiased estimate of the expectation values by learning the noise of each layer of two-qubit gates, then executing an ensemble of circuits that, on average, apply the inverse of that noise. Pauli twirling is used to tailor the noise into a Pauli channel so that it can be learned and inverted efficiently.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
41:["$","p",null,{"children":"In practice, PEC is only as accurate as the learned noise model. The sparse Pauli-Lindblad model includes only one- and two-qubit error terms on neighboring qubits, and it cannot fully capture effects such as crosstalk between distant qubits or time-correlated errors. The device noise can also drift between noise learning and execution. Any noise that the model misses is not canceled, and any noise that the model overestimates is overcompensated, so mitigated results can still deviate from the ideal values in either direction.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
42:["$","p",null,{"children":"TREX is a technique that mitigates measurement errors and is useful for estimating Pauli observable expectation values. It relies on measurement twirling, which diagonalizes the readout-error transfer matrix and makes it easy to invert. A rescaling term is learned from the diagonal noise channel by measuring twirled calibration circuits that are initialized in the zero state.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]

b4:["$","p",null,{"children":"When you submit circuits with multiple configurations, such as with gate twirling, IBM Quantum Compute Service does not necessarily execute all circuit configurations at once. Instead, it groups them into chunks and executes one chunk per shot loop. A shot loop is a single round of execution on the quantum hardware, in which every circuit in the chunk is run for the requested number of shots.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
b5:["$","p",null,{"children":"All jobs submitted to a real QPU go through some classical pre-processing, such as translation into pulses that can be sent to the hardware. Because this pre-processing can be done in parallel, the service dequeues and starts multiple jobs at a time. The QPU, however, can only process one unit of work at a time. This means that if your job generates multiple chunks, its chunks might be interleaved with those of other users' jobs, and each chunk might have to wait for its turn on the QPU.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
